However , these developments offer a very real opportunity for Kenya to close its trade deficit and deliver on the Government 's manufacturing agenda . <p> For manufacturers , innovation and the digital revolution allow traditional players to adapt old business models , offering new products and services to new consumer segments . New technological developments speed up and improve the way innovative products and services are conceived of , produced , accessed and delivered . For consumers , digital transformation has heralded a fundamental shift in their preferences and purchasing habits . <p> Clearly , Kenya must compete with overseas manufacturers on quality and cost , and technology and innovation can help them achieve this . But the manufacturing sector must now also compete in terms of diversity of product if we are to satisfy consumers with increasingly international tastes . All this requires significant investment by Kenyan manufacturers in innovation and digital technologies . <p> In the case of the BAT Group , statements from its CEO suggests that its global restructuring is aimed at freeing up working capital and aligning its internal processes and structures to better meet the evolving preferences of its consumers . To invest further on its endeavor to have what it calls a ' Potentially Reduced Risk Portfolio " of products , it is investing in a Ksh 2.5 billion factory -- the first of its kind on the African continent -- in Nairobi to produce an alternative tobacco-free nicotine product . This is only possible through rapid product innovation advances in societal attitudes and public health awareness . <p> At the Kenya Association of Manufacturers ( KAM ) , we believe that Kenya 's future prosperity relies on embracing innovation . However , investment by manufacturers in technology and innovation alone is not sufficient to get the Manufacturing Agenda back on track . <p> A recent UK Government White Paper on " Regulation for the Fourth Industrial Revolution " ( June 2019 ) identifies six challenges that governments must address to seize the opportunities that innovation presents for their economies . Three of these challenges that underline my point are , first , we need to be on the front foot in reforming regulation in response to technological innovation ; second , we need to ensure that our regulatory system is sufficiently flexible and outcomes-focused to enable innovation to thrive ; and third we need to support innovators to navigate the regulatory landscape and comply with regulations . <p> Lofty ambitions for sure , but Kenya faces more fundamental challenges . More so in respect to the regulatory process and predictability of their outcomes . In part , this is because the core of many of the laws in Kenya , such as Poisons and Pharmacy Act 1956 , Food , Drugs and Chemical Substances Act ( Cap . 254 ) of 1965 and Environmental Management and Coordination Act ( EMCA ) of 1999 need to be urgently reviewed since they do not necessarily reflect the latest innovations or current state of scientific knowledge . <p> A 2018 joint study by KAM and Overseas Development Institute on " how to grow manufacturing and create jobs in a digital economy " , notes that Kenya not only needs to address challenges that affect the manufacturing sector such as high cost of electricity and labour , but also build digital capabilities and manage inclusive digital transformation in through similar targeted actions as those of the UK White paper mentioned above . <p> It is critical that we do not lose our investor-attractiveness shine to countries , which are rapidly reforming their regulatory environments to support future innovations . <p> As Kenyan manufacturers review and look to transform their business models to ensure that they are sustainable and future-fit , the Government too must invest in supporting infrastructure ; further enhancing the ease of doing business ; and , perhaps most importantly , developing robust , transparent and predictable regulatory frameworks that are future-fit and that encourage , rather than stifle , innovation . <p> Regulatory reform is an increasingly important source of competitive advantage in the global economy . It is one that we can ill-afford to ignore . <p> The Writer is the CEO of Kenya Association of Manufacturers and the UN Global Compact Representative for Kenya . She can be reached at ceo@kam.co.ke . <h> Share <p> &gt;https : **33;21908;TOOLONG ... austerity measures will benefit the country in the long runhttps : **36;21943;TOOLONG ... https : **39;21981;TOOLONG ... 08 Oct 2019 14:37:10 +0000https : **28;22022;TOOLONG ? p=842 ... reading " Yatani 's austerity measures will benefit the country in the long run " &gt;A chronic disease has spread throughout the veins of our nation 's elected leadership at many levels . Attention is required right away . How can the government function with such a pervasive sickness ? Those treating it must ensure that no one else comes into contact with it . It must be targeted at its source and eradicated at once . <p> What is ailing so many Kenyan officials ? Corruption and chronic overspending . These are some of the most prevalent issues facing senior public management in the country right now . It is impossible to achieve anything of substance , let alone major development programmes , if each and every leader representing the Kenyan populace in an official capacity is not committed . Serving the Kenyan people is a privilege and an honour with great responsibility , not a job to aspire to for glamorous benefits . <p> Since President Uhuru Kenyatta launched the now famous anti-corruption campaign , leaders have begun to understand that the game is over . Real behavioural change in terms of what we citizens will and will not tolerate is occurring as we speak . People of means no longer see taking a government job as a way to advance their own agenda and use their position of power to make more money . Finally , they must overcome their sickness and take responsibility . <p> But in order to cure the sickness , Uhuru and some of his cabinet secretaries have introduced new measures to help delineate acceptable and unacceptable budgetary allocations . These cost-cutting measures are designed to free up more money for our development programmes . Specifically , projects related to the Big Four Agenda will benefit from the money freed up . <p> Many lavish and unnecessary expenditures will now be more closely regulated and prevented to ensure that every representative of Kenya is living within that state 's means . Acting Treasury Cabinet Secretary Ukur Yatani recently explained the tough restrictions , which include preventing officials from using government vehicles after hours and on weekends . Any official caught using state transport beyond official work hours will pay a surcharge for personal use . Moreover , government ministries will not be able to hold meetings and training sessions in private properties when they can conduct them in government-owned spaces . There is no reason that private offices should be rented and furniture purchased from the state budget when enough government buildings are available to accommodate all ministries . Yatani also urged accounting officials responsible for administering state budgets to find ways for staff to reduce airtime by encouraging internet calls instead . <p> Finally , tighter regulation of foreign and domestic travel will enable some much needed budgetary changes . Often Government Ministers and Principal Secretaries travel abroad in large delegations and opt to fly business or first class -- despite pre-existing regulations against this . Now delegations will be comprised of four representatives maximum , eliminating the extra costs associated with large teams including air travel , accommodation , and per diem expenses . <p> Yatani , a former MP who has temporarily taken on the role of Finance Cabinet Secretary due to the high profile arrest of substantive Treasury CS Henry Rotich , is a great example of the efficacy of the anti-corruption campaign to date . Unlike his predecessor , who allegedly used the treasury position for personal monetary gains , Yatani seems to know how effective prudent financial management can be for both an individual and a nation . <p> We must live within our means , as a state and as individual families . This can be guaranteed through proper financial literacy and regulation . As they witness the anti-corruption campaign and learn how problematic a lack of regulation can be , more Kenyans are becoming aware of why keeping track of money is important , and how beneficial it can be for growth . <p> A mother and father who have two older children and then two younger children later on must plan well ahead if they want to guarantee comfort and a good education for the two youngsters . But if they spend all of their savings on the older two , the others will suffer . It is the same idea in financial planning for a state ; the government must worry about future generations as much as it does about the current one . And finally , it is apparent Kenya has a leader who gets this . Every shilling he saves for us now will be multiplied in value later on . <p> Mr Mugolla comments on topical issues . Email : bmugolla@gmail.com <h> Share <p> &gt;https : **33;22052;TOOLONG ... should tread carefully on food supplements to avoid dopinghttps : **33;22087;TOOLONG ... https : **39;22122;TOOLONG ... 06 Oct 2019 07:54:46 +0000https : **28;22163;TOOLONG ? p=836 ... reading " Athletes should tread carefully on food supplements to avoid doping " &gt;Kenya 's food supplement industry lacks the necessary regulatory framework for it to be declared innocuous . Couple this with the fact the supplements find themselves into chemists , supermarket shelves and training camps under catchy brand names and you have a recipe for disaster in as far as doping is concerned . <p> Athletes , especially those not well informed and sometimes on being misled , can easily test positive for performance-enhancing substances upon consuming these products . <p> The guidelines on the registration of food or dietary supplements and borderline products in Kenya-which were developed in 2012 by the Pharmacy and Poisons Board-define food , dietary or nutritional supplements as products other than tobacco intended to complement the diet . They do not provide any safeguards against contents or usage of these supplements . <p> The guidelines only cater for labeling as long as the supplement has no medical claims on it . In comparison , manufacturers and distributors of dietary supplements and dietary ingredients in the United States of America are prohibited from marketing products that are adulterated or misbranded . <p> Supplements to enhance exercise and athletic performance come in a variety of forms , including tablets , capsules , liquids , powders and bars . Many of these products contain numerous ingredients in varied combinations and amounts , most of which are listed as prohibited substances by the World Anti-Doping Agency ( WADA ) . <p> A study conducted in 2017 ; The Association between Performance and Injury with Dietary Intake and Nutritional Status , among 282 athletes in a training camp in Iten revealed that runners experienced injuries due to inadequate dietary intake of some essential nutrients such as calcium and iron . This could provide a pointer to the reason why supplements are alluring to athletes since they are seen as a quick fix . <p> The Anti-Doping Agency of Kenya ( ADAK ) , just like other leading anti-doping organizations the world over , remind athletes that there are no guarantees that any supplement is free from banned substances . They also reiterate that athletes must be fully aware of the risks to their career if they chose to use them . <p> In other words , the athlete is at liberty to choose whether or not to consume these products on the basis of their understanding of the ' Principle of Strict Liability ' . It states that each athlete is strictly liable for the substances found in his or her body . It is applied in situations where urine or blood samples collected from an athlete have produced adverse analytical results and is not pegged on whether or not the athlete intentionally or unintentionally used a prohibited substance or was negligent or otherwise at fault . <p> The challenges of doping in sport and the growing use of nutritional supplements by athletes are interconnected to the extent that a large number of supplements contain substances that are banned in sport . This is mainly compounded by the fact most of these supplements do not list their ingredients on their packaging thus making them dangerous to an athlete . Years of research have also proven that majority of supplements also contain substances that are associated with substantial health risks . <p> So in consuming these products , especially without a physician 's advice , athletes not only jeopardize their sporting status but also their health . In the government Big Four development program onus has been placed on the achievement of universal health coverage in the country . <p> Several factors within the health sector have been identified as enablers to the achievement of this development agenda . Food security and nutrition together with the focus on preventive healthcare have been identified as some of the enablers to the realization of this pillar . <p> However , doping is one of the threats to the achievement of the health pillar of this grand development agenda . A report by WADA released in September 2018 described Kenyan doping cases as serious and ranked the country as one of the top-three countries with the highest doping cases in the world . This implies that dopers are prone to various health challenges such as cancers , cardiovascular diseases and other terminal illnesses . It follows that with a substantial doping population the achievement of universal health coverage will be threatened by the high cost of treatment associated with such deadly diseases . <p> Scientifically , productivity among a sick population is greatly affected as they can not optimally contribute to the socio-economic development of a country . In addition , dopers are affected socially and economically , and these two aspects also affect the contribution to good health . <p> While it is important to acknowledge that supplement use in sports does not necessarily mean an intention to dope , it is also vital to appreciate that unscrupulous traders will go to any length just to make an extra shilling . Athletes must , therefore , be very cautious when tempted to tinker with some of these products , especially in cases where their contents can not be verified . <p> Given the negative publicity that is initiated and systematically sustained against the country 's athletes on the eve of a major world championship event , Kenyan sportspeople are safer avoiding supplement use just in case they are used as a basis for insinuating their propensity to dope . Simon Mwangi Educator , Anti-Doping Agency of Kenya ( ADAK ) <h> Share <p> &gt;https : **33;22193;TOOLONG ... system with proportional representation good for Kenyahttps : **34;22228;TOOLONG ... https : **39;22264;TOOLONG ... 04 Oct 2019 14:35:23 +0000https : **28;22305;TOOLONG ? p=841 ... reading " Parliamentary system with proportional representation good for Kenya " &gt;A constitutional referendum is looming in Kenya . A key question slowly emerging is whether the country is ripe for a parliamentary system ? And if we should discard the presidential system ? This is certainly an issue of weighty legal and political significance as it implies a need to radically re-configure the State as established under the Constitution of Kenya 2010 . <p> The clamor for a parliamentary system of government , or parliamentary democracy , in Kenya is not new . It has been a recurrent theme throughout the constitutional reform process and after , and was one of the contentious issues at the Bomas Conference of 2004 . <p> At the time , Kenyans voiced a deep desire to rid their country of the all-powerful , autocratic presidency . A parliamentary system , comprising an executive prime minister and a ceremonial president , was then proposed as the perfect solution . However , things changed with the adoption of a presidential system at the 2010 referendum . <p> We have now come full circle . The question we are grappling with today is if the parliamentary system can remedy the real and perceived failures of the presidential system , key among them , lack of inclusivity in national politics . This was evident during the protracted presidential elections in 2017 , when the country was plunged into paroxysms of chaos and violence . <p> The need to curtail this vicious cycle of instability spawned the Building Bridges Initiative ( BRI ) , one of the two main ongoing constitution reform discussions , the other being ' Punguza Mzigo ' . <p> It will be interesting to see what comes out of the two processes . But whatever the outcome(s) , a constitutional referendum is certainly in the offing . The question then becomes , if the country is to change its current governance structure , what would the new system look like ? <p> We need a system that promotes and sustains an inclusive political process at local and national levels . It must also foster unity and entrench a culture of strong political parties with a national outlook . Clearly , the presidential system in Kenya has fallen short of these goals . <p> In my view , a parliamentary democracy offers the next best alternative . But what exactly is parliamentary democracy ? A parliamentary democracy is simply described as a type of government where the citizens directly elect their representatives to the legislature . The representatives through a majority then elect a prime minister who forms and runs the government . <p> Since the legislature can also dismiss the prime minister through a motion of no-confidence , it acts as a check on government on behalf of the people . The executive president under the presidential model is directly elected by the people and becomes head of state and government . The PM in a parliamentary system , on the other hand , is head of government . The head of state in a parliamentary system is either a non-executive president in countries like Italy and Bangladesh , or a **25;22335;TOOLONG monarch in Japan , UK and Sweden . <p> Unlike an executive president who enjoys a defined term limit , the PM may not serve the full term depending on political , social and economic circumstances prevailing in the country . This has happened in some countries , most notably in the UK with the ongoing Brexit fiasco . Majoritarian interests rule the legislature . However , legislators in a parliament democracy are deemed to represent the will of the people in electing or dismissing a government <p> Article 1 of our Constitution articulates a democratic State where the people exercise their sovereign power either directly or through their elected representatives . Kenya is ripe for a parliamentary system with an executive PM elected by MPs , and a non-executive president elected directly by the people . This is known as a parliamentary republican system of government . <p> Given Kenya 's fractious and fragile politics , the parliamentary republican system would work best in our case . But for the parliamentary system to be effective , it must be tied with proportional representation so that every vote counts . The First-Past-The Post electoral system currently in use only serves to perpetuate exclusion by disadvantaging certain groups mostly minorities . <p> The proportional representation system also seeks to ensure the number of seats in a legislative body mirrors the comparative electoral strength of the parties participating in the elections in terms of percentage votes cast . Article 4 of the Constitution provides that Kenya is a multiparty democratic State founded on certain principles espoused in Article 10 to include democracy and participation of the people , inclusiveness , non-discrimination and protection of the marginalized . <p> There is also the aspect of the non-executive or ceremonial president . This would be a person who essentially symbolizes national unity especially in moments of political and constitutional crisis , for example , where the legislature is unable to elect a prime minister occasioning a power vacuum . In such cases , he or she ( non-executive president ) would step in to steady the ship of State . <p> From a Kenyan perspective , the parliamentary republican system of government has many advantages . First , it significantly reduces political tensions in national leadership contests by shifting the actual battle to parliament without necessary supplanting the voters ' universal suffrage rights under Article 38 , since legally , the people delegate the power to elect the prime minister to their elected representatives . <p> In other words , a parliamentary government is still vested with the sovereign will of the people . It is therefore incorrect to argue that the legislature in a parliamentary democracy usurps the power of the people . Instead , it embodies the people 's collective sovereign will in electing the government and its leader . <p> Secondly , adopting parliamentary democracy will serve to strengthen our political party system by promoting a culture of issue-driven politics and coalition building . We need strong political parties with a national outlook and membership cutting across diverse communities as opposed to entities driven by personalities and individual patronage . <p> Thirdly , a parliamentary democracy would ensure a more sustainable political and constitutional order in Kenya compared to the presidential system with its winner-take-all approach that consigns losers in national electoral contests to irrelevance . <p> Besides , a strong opposition within parliament , not outside , would serve as a very effective check on the government . In a mature democracy , parties whether in government or opposition , represent the people effectively by shaping government , law and policy . Parliamentary majorities should not be merely about numbers but also quality of representation . <p> Creating a framework for a parliamentary democracy in Kenya , obviously entails amending several provisions of the Constitution and consequently , various pieces of legislation including the Elections Act , the Independent Electoral and Boundaries Commission ( IEBC ) Act , the Political Parties Act to name a few . This is because the Constitution under Chapter 7 provides for an electoral process at three levels namely , the electoral system , IEBC and political parties . <p> We also need to amend Chapter 7 of the Constitution to entrench proportional representation as the guiding framework for representation of the people , and the electoral system . <p> Civic education is also required to disabuse Kenyans of the notion that a non-executive president is ' toothless and ineffective ' and that the people should only elect a powerful president . Such is a self-serving narrative peddled by politicians for ulterior motives . Moreover , history teaches that a powerful presidency or Executive is not always a good thing as the same can be divisive and a threat to democracy , freedom , inclusivity and unity . <p> The fear that a parliamentary system only serves to create an impotent , ceremonial president is simply misplaced . A non-executive president should be a symbol of national unity , a respected statesman or woman , who is not perceived as a prisoner of vested party or regional interests . There are many competent , experienced , respected men and women in Kenya fit for the role . <p> Mr Mwachinga is an Advocate of the High Court of Kenya emwachinga@yahoo.co.uk <h> Share <p> &gt;https : **33;22362;TOOLONG ... confidence in women in his govt proves they can make a good presidenthttps : **30;22397;TOOLONG ... https : **39;22429;TOOLONG ... 03 Oct 2019 05:34:52 +0000https : **28;22470;TOOLONG ? p=840 ... reading " Uhuru 's confidence in women in his govt proves they can make a good president " &gt;There is a glass ceiling in Kenya , and while many focus on the ethnic and tribal element , I would argue that it is a gender-based ceiling . <p> While some of our neighbours in Africa , like Ethiopia , Malawi and Liberia , have had female presidents , for many generations the thought of a woman president was only a dream . <p> Is all that about to change ? <p> As we have seen , change in Africa in general and in Kenya in particular takes time , but we would be in denial if we do not see it budding all over our nation in recent years . <p> Perhaps the most prominent example of some glass ceilings being shattered is the role of Ambassador Raychelle Awour Omamo SC as the Cabinet Secretary Ministry of State for Defense . <p> Ambassador Omamo is the first woman to hold a position traditionally the bastion of men , and by all accounts she is doing an excellent job . <p> An argument often made to exclude women from this position is their supposed lack of knowledge and experience in defense and security areas , because of the paucity of high-ranking women in the KDF . " <p> I believe it is no coincidence that he specifically chose an event for girls and young women .
